Secondary Metabolites from the
Bioconversion of Banana (<em>Musa balbisiana</em>) Peel-Derived
Biomass with <em>Aspergillus niger</em>. Metabolomic Insights
into the Chemical Profiles</h1></hgroup><div class="cg p">

<div id="anp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none"><div class="notes p"><section id="historyarticle-meta1" class="history"><p>Received 2025 Oct 14; Accepted 2026 Jan 6; Revised 2025 Dec 27; Collection date 2026 Feb 3.</p></section></div></div>
<div id="clp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none">
<div>© 2026 The Authors. Published by American Chemical Society</div>
<p>This article is licensed under CC-BY 4.0</p>
<div class="p"><a href="/about/copyright/" class="usa-link">PMC Copyright notice</a></div>
</div>
</div>
<div>PMCID: PMC12878763  PMID: <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41658150/" class="usa-link">41658150</a>
</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p>Santa Catarina is
Brazil’s fourth-largest banana
producer,
generating a significant amount of waste. Banana peel conversion into
useful products is uncommon. However, it produces natural products
that possess benefits for human health or can serve as biomass to
produce fungi-derived metabolites. Therefore, <em>Aspergillus
niger</em> was isolated from banana peels and then cultured on
three distinct media composed of starch, banana peels (BP), and starch-enriched
banana peels (BPS) for 14, 21, and 28 days at 28 °C. Their chemical
profiles were established using liquid chromatography coupled to mass
spectrometry data assisted by the GNPS database and MS-FINDER software.
In BP media, 76 secondary metabolites were detected; among them, 66
were produced by the fungus. Twenty-seven compounds, such as nigragillin
and pyranonigrin A, are <em>A. niger</em> chemomarkers, while
other substances, except for the 13 unidentified, were previously
found in other organisms. Among the 99 metabolites detected in BPS
media, 22 were previously identified as <em>A. niger</em>-derived
compounds. BPS media also yielded 26 unidentified compounds, while
12 annotated ones were also found in BP. The remaining metabolites
were previously reported in other organisms. Principal component 1
of 44.8% and principal component 2 of 22.2% indicated that 21-day
fermentation for BP medium and 28-day fermentation for BPS medium
were the optimal conditions to produce secondary metabolites. Variable
importance plots reveal nonadecan-2-amine (score &gt;3) and an alkylaryl
amine derivative (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 304.3005)
as the most concentrated compounds. The present results showed a cheap
process to turn food residues and waste into valuable molecules.</p></section><section class="abstract" id="abstract2"><hr class="headless">
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/bd61/12878763/19f768607bd8/ao5c10614_0011.jpg" loading="lazy" id="abs1" height="992" width="3475" alt="graphic file with name ao5c10614_0011.jpg"></p></section><section class="abstract" id="abstract3"><hr class="headless">
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/bd61/12878763/19f768607bd8/ao5c10614_0010.jpg" loading="lazy" id="tgr1" height="992" width="3475" alt="graphic file with name ao5c10614_0010.jpg"></p></section><section id="sec1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">1. Introduction</h2>
<p>Growing urbanization and
lifestyle changes have colossally increased
waste production, especially food residue and waste (FRW) from industries,
agriculture, and households. According to the UN Environment Program,
1052 million tons of organic waste/residue worldwide were from food,
with almost 2% produced in Brazil in 2022.<em>
,
</em>
</p>
<p>Based on the report from the Brazilian Institute of Geography and
Statistics, a unit of the Brazilian Ministry of Planning and Budget,
Santa Catarina state in 2024 was the fourth largest producer of bananas
in Brazil (IBGE, 2024). Banana peel residues
account for 14% of waste in banana farming. Poor management of these wastes can cause environmental impacts
(gas emissions, contaminated groundwater, and noxious odors) and consequences
for economic income associated with natural resources such as water
and land. A variety of sustainable techniques are currently used to
reduce the impact of FRW. Although in some cases FRW is used to produce
energy, compost, animal feed, and biofertilizers, in others, it is
sent to landfills or incinerated. Other strategies, such as biotechnology
processes that produce valuable bioactive compounds from FRW, contribute
to the bioeconomy initiative. These bioactive compounds can be turned
into food supplements or food ingredients known to possess health
benefits; they can also contribute to drug research and discovery. Therefore, FRW can undergo organic solvent extraction
and purification to identify these valuable substances. More effective management and wider integration
into economic, research, and agricultural sectors are important to
mitigate the impact of FRW, particularly banana peels. As shown in
previous studies, banana peels have nutritive values and can be considered
a functional food because of their content of minerals (iron, calcium,
magnesium, and sodium), amino acids (valine, threonine, and phenylalanine),
phenolic acids (gallic, tannic, ferulic, and caffeic acids), flavonoids
(rutin and quercetin), carotenoids (β- and α-carotene),
terpenoids (stigmasterol), and fatty acids (palmitic acid).<em>
,
</em>
</p>
<p>Thus, banana peels can also be used as biomass for microbial
fermentation
to generate high-value products. This
last process is known to produce flavoring for fermented beverages;
lactic, citric, and succinic acids; enzymes (lipase, amylase, cellulase,
and others); ethanol; and hydrogen.<em>
−



</em> A previous study reported that solid- and liquid-state
cultivation of <em>Aspergillus niger</em> using orange peels
as a substrate produced protease. In
addition, <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. grown on cassava peel,
groundnut shell, or wheat bran-based solid-state cultures produced
neomycin, oxytetracycline, and meroparamycin.<em>
,
</em> Therefore, the use of FRW as biomass is fundamental for a circular
bioeconomy. Based on the aforementioned information, this work aimed
to investigate the chemical response of <em>A. niger</em> when
grown on a biomass composed of banana peels. The species <em>A.
niger</em> was selected for this study because it is an airborne
fungus with wide application in industrial biotechnology and because
it is prevalent in household waste used for biomass, particularly
in banana peels used in this work. Experiments using <em>A. niger</em> cultivated on FRW biomasses such as wheat bran, coffee pulp, pineapple
canary waste, tomato waste, and apple pomace produced citric acid,
lycopene, biosorbents, vanillic acid, vanillin, and polygalacturonase. The profile of secondary metabolites varies
according to the type of FRW, representing an advantage in the identification
of new byproducts and valuable chemical substances with potential
applications in drug discovery and as bioinoculants, fungicides, and
biostimulants, among others. Previous studies used banana peels as
biomass to grow <em>A. niger</em> to produce bioethanol, citric
acid, and enzymes such as xylanase, amylase, and pectinase.<em>
−

</em>
</p>
<p>In general, the use of food waste for fungal cultivation not
only
produces valuable substances but also represents an economical process
when compared with conventional techniques that produce metabolites
derived from microorganisms using purchased items, including biomass
(rice, agar, and starch) and other nutrients. In this study, the hypothesis
was to investigate whether organic waste, such as banana peels, can
serve as biomass for microorganisms, enabling the biosynthesis of
biologically relevant compounds. Furthermore, emphasis was placed
on the secondary metabolites of banana peel and their impact on the
chemical response of <em>A. niger</em>.</p>
<p>To date, secondary
metabolites generated by <em>A. niger</em> grown on biomass
made of banana peels have never been investigated.</p>
<p>We herein
report a comparative study using the dereplication technique
and statistical tools to analyze LC-MS chemical profiles of <em>A. niger</em> cultivated on banana peels, starch-enriched banana
peel, and starch. In addition to the culture media used for <em>A. niger</em> growth, fermentation periods of 14, 21, and 28
days were also used as comparison parameters.</p></section><section id="sec2"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">2. Materials and Methods</h2>
<section id="sec2.1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1. Materials</h3>
<p>Acetonitrile, methanol,
water, isopropanol (LCMS grade), and leucine entkephalin were obtained
from Servylab, a Sigma-Aldrich seller (Rio Grande do Sul, Brazil).
The syringe filter (0.22 μM, hydrophobic), vials (2 mL), and
hydrophobic and hydrophilic filter membranes for solvent filtration
were furnished by Filtrilo (Paraná, Brazil). Ethyl acetate
of analytical grade was furnished by Rauter (Gravata, Rio Grande do
Sul).</p></section><section id="sec2.2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2. Fungus Collection and Characterization</h3>
<p>Banana peels (<em>Musa balbisiana</em>), popularly known
as “banana <em>figo</em>”, were purchased from
a local market in the city of Florianópolis, Santa Catarina,
Brazil. To reproduce how microorganisms colonize and consume organic
matter in nature and to make sure that the selected microorganism
will grow on this biomass, the peels were washed in running water
to remove contaminants, and then, a quantity (5 g) was ground in 50
mL of distilled water in a beaker. The beaker containing this organic
material was kept in the lab for 7 days until colonies of microorganisms
appeared. The predominant fungus was isolated on Potato Dextrose Agar
plates and grown for 21 days at 28 °C. Further, the strain was
identified by micromorphological characterization, applying the adapted
Riddell technique, and confirmed by the
amplification and sequencing technique of the ITS gene (ITS region:
ITS1), performed by the biotechnology company Neoprospecta in Florianópolis,
Santa Catarina, Brazil. The sequence was registered in the NCBI database
under GenBank number <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PX619933" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PX619933</a>.</p></section><section id="sec2.3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3. Substrate
Preparation and Inoculation</h3>
<p>The spore suspension used as
inoculum was prepared by adding Tween
80 (0.01% v/v to the fungus colony previously cultivated on BDA plates
at 28 °C for 7 days, following the protocol described by Robl
et al. (2015). The spore counting was
performed by using a Neubauer chamber. Initially, the concentrated
suspension was diluted 10x in an Eppendorf tube. After homogenization
using a vortex mixer, two 20 μL aliquots of the diluted solution
were transferred to the chamber. By using a microscope with 40×
magnification, the spores present in the five diagonal squares of
the central quadrant of the chamber were counted. The spore concentration
in the suspension was calculated using the following equation:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="ueq1"><tr><td class="formula"><math id="d1e349" display="block"><mi>concentration</mi><mspace width=".25em"></mspace><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>spores</mi><mo>·</mo><msup><mrow><mi>mL</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>dilution factor</mi><mo>·</mo><mn>2.5</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mrow><mn>10</mn></mrow><mrow><mn>5</mn></mrow></msup><mo>·</mo><mi>spore count</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>5</mn></mrow></mfrac></math></td></tr></table>
<p>The spore suspension was diluted in
order to reach the desired final concentration of 10<sup>6</sup> spores·mL<sup>–1</sup> in the culture medium.</p>
<p>Three flasks received
a ratio of 50 g of banana peel to 1 L of
deionized water as the culture medium. These flasks were covered with
cotton and autoclaved at 121 °C, 1 atm for 30 min. Soon after, at room temperature and within a
laminar flow, the inoculum of <em>A. niger</em> was added
to the culture media and incubated in B.O.D. at 28 °C. A similar
procedure was performed with starch in a concentration of 50 g/L.</p>
<p>The mixture of starch and banana peels to formulate an alternative
culture medium was motivated by previous works whose results indicate
that varying carbohydrate sources (such as sucrose, lactose, mannitol,
potato dextrose, flour, and starch) can influence fungal growth and
the biosynthesis of specific biomolecules.<em>
,
</em> Thus, the impact of supplementing the banana peel biomass medium
with starch as a carbon source was assessed in relation to the profile
of secondary metabolites. Starch quantity was not assessed as a culture
parameter, but literature suggests 20 g/L is optimal for <em>A.
niger</em> growth. Thus, a 1:1 (w/w)
banana peel/starch mix totaling 50 g in 1 L of distilled water to
cultivate the fungus was prepared in three different flasks. The cultivation
was carried out in the periods of 14, 21, and 28 days at a constant
temperature of 28 °C. Thus, three culture media containing only
banana peel (<strong>BP_An</strong>), three containing banana peels enriched
with starch (<strong>BPS_An</strong>), and three containing starch (<strong>Starch</strong>) were obtained.</p></section><section id="sec2.4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.4. Preparation
of Crude Extracts</h3>
<p>After
each time of fungus cultivation, ethyl acetate was added to stop the
fungus growth and perform the extraction. The organic extract was
obtained with 200 mL of ethyl acetate assisted by sonication in an
ultrasonic bath for 1 h, following the solid–liquid extraction
process; these steps were repeated three times for each medium, thus
obtaining a total of 6 extracts. The extracts were concentrated by
rotary evaporation. As intended to obtain a blank for the extracts,
this same extraction procedure was applied to samples from inoculated
starch, noninoculated banana peel (<strong>BP</strong>), and noninoculated
starch-enriched banana peels. The aim was to discriminate banana-peel-
and starch-derived metabolites from those produced by the fungus.</p></section><section id="sec2.5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.5. Ultra-Performance Liquid Chromatography-Tandem
Mass Spectrometry Analysis (UPLC-ESI-MS/MS)</h3>
<p>The obtained
extracts were prepared by dissolving 1.6 mg in 2 mL of methanol/acetonitrile
(1:1 v/v, LC-MS grade), resulting in a concentration of 800 μg/mL.
Before analysis, all extracts were filtered through a 0.22 μm
hydrophilic PTFE filter (Analytical).</p>
<p>An Acquity UPLC system
class H (Waters Co., USA) equipped with a photodiode array detector
(PDA), sample manager, and a quaternary solvent manager as well as
a BEH C18 column (50 mm, 1.0 mm, particle size 1.7 μm (Waters))
was used for the separation. The column and the sample tray were maintained
at temperatures of 40 and 20 °C, respectively. Each sample (2
μL) was injected and analyzed under gradient conditions at a
flow rate of 0.3 mL/min. The mobile phase was composed of H<sub>2</sub>O containing 0.1% formic acid, pH 3.0 (A) and LC-MS-grade acetonitrile
(B). The gradient was the following: between 0 and 1 min, 90% A/10%
B; between 1 and 12 min, 10% A/90% B; between 12 and 14 min, 10% A/90%
B; between 14 and 15 min, 90% A/10% B; and between 15 and 20 min,
the initial mixture was applied to equilibrate the column.</p>
<p>A
mass spectrometer, Xevo G2-S QTof (Waters), bearing an electrospray
ionization (ESI) probe operating in positive and negative ionization
modes, coupled to the UPLC device was used to detect the chemical
components of the extracts. Nitrogen was used as nebulizer gas; chromatograms
were acquired with the cone gas flow of 100 L/h, the desolvation gas
flow of 900 L/h, the sampling cone current of 40 V, and the source
offset current of 80 V. The collision gas was argon, and the lockspray
reference sample was leucine enkephalin with reference mass values
at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 554.2615 (ESI−) and
556.2771 (ESI+). The desolvation and the ionization source were maintained
during the analyses at 250 and 90 °C, respectively, while the
capillary voltage was 3 kV. A range of 30–35 eV was used as
the collision energy. Data were acquired over a range of 50–1500
Da in positive mode and 50–1500 Da in negative mode, at a scan
time of 1.0 s over 20 min, and were processed with MassLynx V4.1 (Waters).
The MS data were acquired in Fast Data-Dependent acquisition (Fast
DDA) and MS<sup>E</sup> (DIA) settings using argon as collision gas
and applying the energy range of 25–35 eV (<a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acsomega.5c10614/suppl_file/ao5c10614_si_001.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Supporting Information</a>).</p></section><section id="sec2.6"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.6. Data
Processing by MS DIAL/MS FINDER</h3>
<p>A combination of MS-DIAL
(version 4.92) and MS-FINDER (version 3.52)
software was used to assist in establishing the chemical profile.
The raw data from the Fast DDA analyses were loaded directly into
MS-DIAL, where the information was selected according to the analyses
performed by UPLC-MS, and sections for data processing and alignment
were selected. The values applied to the parameters were: tolerance
of 0.02 and 0.03 Da for MS1 and MS2, respectively; peak detection
amplitude of 10000 for the minimum peak height and 0.1 Da for the
mass cutoff width; for deconvolution, a sigma window value of 0.5
and an MS/MS abundance cutoff of 30 amplitudes; the retention time
tolerance was set to 0.5 min and 0.02 Da for MS<sup>1</sup> tolerance.
The option to remove features based on solvent blank data was also
applied, thus obtaining annotations referring only to the samples.
</p>
<p>The data obtained in MS-DIAL were exported
to MS-FINDER, where the identification of features was provided by
comparing experimental MS/MS fragmentation spectra from several spectral
databases (COCONUT, PubChem, UNPD, ChEBI, NANPDB, KNApSAcK, NPA).
To select the structure as a potential candidate, a score value greater
than or equal to 5 among the fragments obtained and knowledge of the
possible classes of compounds that the sample type can produce were
considered.
</p>
<p>The features that did
not lead to structure proposals by the software
were subjected to the dereplication technique using MS<sup>E</sup> fragmentation data in comparison with those reported in the literature.
The molecular formulas were determined by using the MassLynx elemental
composition tool. Each molecular formula was chosen with a tolerance
lower than or equal to ±5 ppm between the calculated and measured
mass values.</p></section><section id="sec2.7"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.7. Molecular Network by GNPS</h3>
<p>The raw
Fast-DDA data acquired by UPLC-MS for the extracts were converted
into the corresponding format (mzML) using MSConvert software (ProteoWizard,
Palo Alto, CA, USA) and uploaded to the GNPS online platform using
Core FTP LE software v. 2.2. Molecular network analysis was performed
following the online workflow (<a href="https://ccms-ucsd.github.io/GNPSDocumentation/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://ccms-ucsd.github.io/GNPSDocumentation/</a>) described on the GNPS web site. Molecular
networks were obtained using a precursor ion mass tolerance of 0.02
Da and an MS/MS fragment ion tolerance of 0.03 Da. Network edges were
produced only with a cosine score above 0.77 and a minimum match of
4 peaks in the fragmentation spectrum. The spectral data in the network
were then compared with the GNPS spectral libraries. For matches of
the GNPS library with the data, a score above 0.7 and at least 4 matching
peaks were applied. The generated molecular networks were visualized
using Cytoscape software, version 3.10.2.</p></section><section id="sec2.8"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.8. Statistical
Analysis</h3>
<p>For statistical
analysis, peak intensity data deconvolved by MS-DIAL were extracted
and reorganized into a table in CSV format. Univariate and multivariate
statistical analyses were performed on the MetaboAnalyst 6.0 online
platform (<a href="https://www.metaboanalyst.ca/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://www.metaboanalyst.ca/</a>) to discriminate significant metabolites between the culture media
(BP_An, BPS_An, noninoculated BP and starch), as well as the metabolites
at different growth times (14, 21, and 28 days). Data were normalized
by square root transformation and Pareto scaling. Principal component
analysis (PCA) and partial least squares discriminant analysis (PLS-DA)
were used to characterize the distribution of the data set and determine
the main metabolites responsible for the distribution, the latter
based on the thresholds of the variable importance in projection (VIP)
value &gt;1 and <em>p</em>-value &lt;0.05. Heat plots were
applied
to visualize the relationship between the samples and the abundances
of the compounds in each sample.</p></section></section><section id="sec3"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">3. Results
and Discussion</h2>
<section id="sec3.1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.1. Fungal Isolation and Cultivation</h3>
<p>The isolated strain was identified through micromorphological features
as an <em>Aspergillus</em> species (<a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Figure 
</a> and <a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acsomega.5c10614/suppl_file/ao5c10614_si_001.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Figure S1–S3</a>), applying an adapted Riddell technique. Using the slide microcultivation technique, it was possible to verify
the microscopic characteristics of the fungal isolate under an optical
microscope. Classic characteristics of the fungus <em>A. niger</em> were observed, including abundant black conidiophores and conidia
and a smooth hyaline wall.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig1"><h4 class="obj_head">1.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12878763_ao5c10614_0001.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/bd61/12878763/a535cf8df31e/ao5c10614_0001.jpg" loading="lazy" id="gr1" height="546" width="681" alt="1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Micromorphological aspect of the fungus evaluated
at 7 and 14 days
of cultivation (micro cultivation). (A–C) 7 days of micro cultivation,
(D–F) 14 days of micro cultivation. (A and D) 20× magnification;
(B, C, E. F) 40× magnification.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Molecular identification using amplification and
sequencing techniques
of the ITS gene region (ITS1) matched the strain of <em>Aspergillus
niger</em> with 99% homology. Sequencing of this region of the
fungus, renamed <em>Aspergillus niger</em> LQPN001, resulted
in the following sequence:</p>
<p>TTACCGAGTGCGGGTCCTTTGGGC​CCAACCTC​CCATC​CGT​GTC​TAT​TGTA​CCCTG​TTG​CTT​CGC​GGAC​CCG​CCG​CTT​GTC​GGC​CGC​CGG​GGG​GGC​GCC​TCT​GCCC​CCC​GGG​CCC​GTG​CCC​GCC​GGA​GAC​CCC​AAC​ACG​AACAC​TGT​CTG​AAA​GCGT​GCA​GTC​TGA​GTT​GA​TTGA​AT​GCA​ATC​AGT​TAA​AAC​TTT​CAA​CAA​TGG​ATCT​CTT​GGT​TCCG</p>
<p>The BLASTN search resulted in four species with 99.54% similarity
of the ITS genetic code, all with E-value of 2–111 and 100%
coverage of the searched gene: <em>Aspergillus fetidus</em> (CBS 121.28), <em>Aspergillus awamori</em> ATCC 16877 (CBS
557.65), <em>Aspergillus niger</em> ATCC 16888 (CBS 554.65),
and <em>Aspergillus welwitschiae</em> (CBS:139.54).</p>
<p>The fungus, <em>A. niger</em>, was grown on <em>Musa
balbisiana</em> banana peels, starch-enriched peels, and starch
for 14, 21, and 28 days, producing nine crude extracts: BP_An14d,
BP_An21d, BP_An28d, BPS_An14d, BPS_An21d, BPS_An28d, Starch_An14d,
Starch_An21d, and Starch_An28d. The chemical profiles of these crude
extracts, along with those obtained from banana peels, were established
by using LCMS data. The LCMS profile of the starch-enriched medium
without inoculation was similar to that of banana peels. However,
this profile showed low sensitivity due to the low concentration of
metabolites.</p></section><section id="sec3.2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2. Statistical Analysis</h3>
<p>To perform a
comprehensive comparison correlating all variables and determining
the differences between culture media and cultivation times, as well
as identifying the main metabolites responsible for this variation,
multivariate analyses were applied. Data obtained from the positive
and negative ionization modes were used separately for the comparison.</p>
<p>Principal component analysis (PCA) revealed significant differences
between the chemical profiles of the samples, forming clusters, as
depicted in <a href="#fig2" class="usa-link">Figure 
</a>. The first two principal components (PC1 and PC2) explained 65%
and 76.7% of the total variance of the data obtained from the positive
and negative mode analyses, respectively. These PC values best represent
the correlation between the groups.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig2"><h4 class="obj_head">2.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12878763_ao5c10614_0002.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/bd61/12878763/099885b7a344/ao5c10614_0002.jpg" loading="lazy" id="gr2" height="348" width="730" alt="2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>PCA plot with metabolites identified in
the extracts obtained from
the cultivation of the fungus <em>A. niger</em> in BP, BPS,
and starch for 14, 21, and 28 days of growth in positive (ESI+) and
negative (ESI−) ionization modes.</p></figcaption></figure><p>The PCA plot in positive mode analysis showed no
proximity of BP_An21d
to other samples. In contrast, it was grouped in a negative mode with
BP and BPS samples except for BPS_An28d. This difference suggests
that the BP_An21d sample contains metabolites that behave differently
according to the ionization modes. Additionally, the BPS_An28d sample
stood out for presenting a unique profile in both ionization modes.
This sample was alone in the cluster, indicating a significantly different
chemical composition profile. All samples from the starch medium formed
a cluster in both ionization modes and were far from the BP and BPS
samples.</p>
<p>The statistical analysis focused on a list of MS peaks
and their
corresponding areas ranked using <em>t</em> tests. From this
analysis, the top 15 metabolites identified through the variable importance
in projection (VIP) from partial least-squares discriminant analysis
(PLS-DA) in both positive and negative modes were selected for correlation
in the heatmap. The VIP plot produced by the PLS-DA models ranked
metabolites based on their ability to differentiate between sample
groups, including the biomass sample and those obtained from various
fermentation times. The color scale (ranging from blue to red) indicates
the relative amounts of each metabolite across various sample groups
(red indicates a high quantity, and blue is not present). Previously
identified metabolites are shown with their compound names, while
additional metabolites are indicated by NI (Non Identified) followed
by their precursor ion (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em>) found
on the left side of the plot. The sample groups were based on fermentation
media (starch and banana peels medium, BP; and banana peel medium
enriched with starch, BPS) and duration (14, 21, and 28 d), are labeled
at the bottom of the plot.</p>
<p>The VIP plot reveals nonadecan-2-amine
(score &gt;3) and the alkylaryl
amine derivative, <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 304.3005 (score
&gt;2.5) as the most concentrated compounds in the positive mode ionization
(<a href="#fig3" class="usa-link">Figure 
</a>). In contrast,
it shows the fatty acid derivative (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 311.221) (score &gt;1.8) and the monoglyceride disaccharide (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 559.3123) (score = 1.8) as the most
prevalent in the negative mode ionization.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig3"><h4 class="obj_head">3.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12878763_ao5c10614_0003.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/bd61/12878763/7bbee480114d/ao5c10614_0003.jpg" loading="lazy" id="gr3" height="339" width="700" alt="3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Fifteen most important
metabolites from partial least squares discriminant
analysis variable importance (PLS-DA VIP) from the cultivation of
the fungus <em>A. niger</em> in BP, BPS, and starch at 14,
21, and 28 days of growth in positive (ESI+) and negative (ESI−)
ionization modes.</p></figcaption></figure><p>The heatmap in <a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">Figure 
</a>, displaying the
top 60 metabolites based
on the ANOVA <em>p</em>-value, was created using MetaboAnalyst
5.0 to forecast
the distribution of metabolites during the fermentation timeline of
14, 21, and 28 days. In positive mode ionization, the chemical profile
of BPS_An14d was close to that of banana peels (BP). Both were hierarchically
close to BPS_An21d. The sequence of similarity in the hierarchy was
followed by banana peel medium fermented for 28 days (BP_An28d), then
BP_An14d, BP_An21d, BPS_An28d, Starch_An21d, Starch_An14d, and Starch_An28d.
As observed in the PCA plot, fermentation periods of 21 to 28 days
were suitable for <em>A. niger</em> to produce highly diverse
secondary metabolites when grown on the banana peel and starch-enriched
banana peel media, respectively. Moreover, a fermentation time of
28 days was the best for this fungus to produce more natural products.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig4"><h4 class="obj_head">4.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12878763_ao5c10614_0004.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/bd61/12878763/6bdc1fb4233f/ao5c10614_0004.jpg" loading="lazy" id="gr4" height="510" width="700" alt="4"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig4/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Heat map
plot with 60 top metabolites identified in the extracts
obtained from the cultivation of the fungus <em>A. niger</em> in BP, BPS, and starch at 14, 21, and 28 days of growth in positive
(ESI+) and negative (ESI−) ionization mode.</p></figcaption></figure><p>The hierarchical distribution was different for
the negative mode
of ionization. Four groups were observed; among them, the first was
composed of BP_An21d, BP_an14d, and BP_An28d. This group was close
to BPS_An21d, BPS_An14d, and BP. Both clusters were close in hierarchy
to BPS_An28d. The samples Starch_An21d, Starch_An14d, and Starch_An28d
formed a group that was the end of the hierarchy. The present analysis
also revealed that the fungus grown on media prepared with banana
peels, produced in all periods, is a secondary metabolite sensitive
to the negative ionization mode. However, a fermentation time of 28
days was optimal for significantly producing fungus-derived metabolites
on the starch-enriched banana peel medium.</p></section><section id="sec3.3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.3. Dereplication
of the Chemical Profiles</h3>
<p>Crude extracts obtained from the
fermentation, as well as those from
banana peels and starch, were subjected to UPLC-ESI-QTOF-MS analysis.
Generated mass spectrometry data (<a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acsomega.5c10614/suppl_file/ao5c10614_si_001.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Figures S4–S9</a>) were converted into a compatible format and then processed using
MS-Dial, MS-FINDER, and GNPS.</p>
<p>The data obtained are compiled
in <a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">Tables 
</a>–, in which the structures proposed by the aforementioned
software and databases received scores from 0 to 10 (highest rate).
Structure assignments without a score were possible using the dereplication
technique in conjunction with previous studies reported in the literature.
LCMS data from banana peels (BP) and samples from the fermentation
of <em>A. niger</em> on banana peels for 14 (BP_An14d), 21
(BP_An21d), and 28 (BP_An28d) days revealed the presence of 76 secondary
metabolites, among them, 10 substances were found in BP. The structures
of banana peel metabolites were assigned as a polyol (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 261.1316), piptamine (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 332.3312), nonadecan-2-amine (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 284.3316), linolenic acid derivative (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 279.2327), isoorientin (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 471.1050), eriodyctiol-6-C-beta-<span class="font-variant-small-caps">d</span>-glucopyranoside
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 473.1027), di­(-2-ethylhexyl)­phathalate
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 391.2848), icosalide B (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 685.4361), di­(2-ethylhexyl)­phthalate
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 413.2653), and cycloeucalenone
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 425.3781). In addition to secondary
metabolites from banana peels, twenty-seven others were previously
reported in <em>A. niger</em>,<em>
,
</em> while 18 were
formerly described in other <em>Aspergillus</em> species.<em>
,
</em> Nigragillin was found at 1.63 min with a <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> of 223.1811, and the same mass value also appeared at
0.76 min, suggesting either a pyrazine or a piperazine derivative.
The low fragmentation limits the consistent characterization of this
secondary metabolite. However, pyrazines such as 2-methoxy-3,5-diisobutylpyrazine,
2-methoxy-3,6-di<em>sec</em>-butylpyrazine, and 2-methoxy-3,6-diisobutylpyrazine,
with the same elemental composition, were obtained from the bacteria, <em>Carpophilus humeralis</em>. Pyranonigrin
A (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 224.0557) together with other
components, namely, phenoxyacetic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 153.0553), cycloleucomelone (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 339.0503), atromentin (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 323.0550), carbonarone A (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 230.0819),
pyrophen (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 310.1057), nigerpyrone
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 187.0759), hexylcitric acid
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 275.1126), orlandin (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 411.1079), pestalamide A (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 344.1132), demethylkotanin (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 425.1232), aspernigrin B (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 457.1759), hexylitaconic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 213.1126), nigerasperone C (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 575.1550), kotanin and its isomer (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 439.1388), aurasperone D (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 555.1280), flavasperone (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 287.0920),
fonsecinone B (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 589.1706), nipyrone
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 261.1464), and aurasperone
A (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 571.1605) previously identified
in <em>A. niger</em> were also found in this study.<em>
,
</em> Most of the naphtho-γ-pyrones were detected alongside their
positional or functional isomers (<a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">Table 
</a>).</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/tbl3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p>Part of the other metabolites produced by <em>A. niger</em> in this study were previously reported in other <em>Aspergillus</em> species. Ochramide B, 2,10-dimethyl 4-hydroxy-6-oxo-4-undecen-7-yne
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 209.1541), similanpyrone B
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 207.0657), a pyrazine, a β-hydroxyenone,
and an isocoumarin, produced respectively by <em>A. ochraceus</em>
<em>
,
</em> and <em>A. similanensis</em>, were detected in the present study. Moreover,
PsiAβ (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 297.2428) and PsiAα
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 293.2116), previously obtained
from <em>A. nidulans,</em> were also detected in this study. Other secondary metabolites, including asperlone
B, Sch725674 (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 351.2143), asperfuranone
A (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 259.1312), a 12,13-dihydroxyoctadec-9-enoic
acid derivative (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 337.2350),
8,11,12-trihydroxyoctadec-9-enoic acid derivative (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 313.2373), proversilin A (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 273.1468), 9,10-dihydroxystearic acid derivative (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 339.2511), 6-keto stearic acid derivative
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 299.2588), 9-oxooctadeca-10,12,15-trienoic
acid derivative (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 291.1952),
linolenic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 279.2324), and <em>N</em>-[2-Hydroxy-1-(hydroxymethyl)­octadecyl]­hexadecanamide (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 554.5506) formerly identified in a
partially sequenced <em>Aspergillus</em> were also characterized.</p>
<p>Based on literature searches, two of the annotated compounds, thelephoric
acid and 3-hydroxy-4-(6-methoxy-5,8-dioxonaphthalen-2-yl)-5-oxofuran-2-ylidene]­(4-hydroxyphenyl)­acetic
acid, were previously identified in the mushrooms, <em>Polyozellus
multiplex</em>
 and <em>Xerocomus
badius</em>, respectively. This study
also detected botcinic acid and a harzianumol derivative, previously
identified in the fungi <em>Botrytis cinerea</em> and <em>Trichoderma harzianum</em>, respectively.<em>
,
</em> In addition, menaquinone 7 and salinipyrone A, two bacteria-derived
metabolites,<em>
,
</em> were detected in the <em>A. niger</em> profile.</p>
<p>
<a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">Table 
</a> also displays
metabolites that were not described in any microorganisms, which include
2-methyl-<em>N</em>-(2-phenylethyl)­propanamide (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 192.1386), 2-methyl-<em>N</em>-(2-phenylethyl)­butanamide
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 206.1546), 8,11,12-trihydroxyoctadec-9-enoic
acid derivative (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 353.2297),
a nor-diterpene (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 263.2376),
two isomers of 4α,14α-dimethyl-24-methylene-cholest-7,9(11)-dien-3β-ol
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 425.3776), two isomeric triterpenes
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 457.3662), apocholic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 391.2849), and <em>cis</em>/<em>trans</em>-erucamide derivatives (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 338.3422).</p>
<p>The structure assignment of
part of the proposed compounds was
checked to sustain the MS-FINDER proposal by using the reported fragmentation
pattern of ions listed in <a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acsomega.5c10614/suppl_file/ao5c10614_si_001.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Tables S1 and S2</a>. <a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acsomega.5c10614/suppl_file/ao5c10614_si_001.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Schemes S1–S6</a> display the structure of fragment ions that led
us to the chemical profile in <a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">Tables 
</a> and .</p>

<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="t2fn1">
<sup>b</sup><p class="display-inline">Metabolites identified in both substrates,
NI: not identified.</p>
</div></div></section><p>The
peak at a retention time (tR) of 1.63 min with <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 223.1811 [C<sub>13</sub>H<sub>22</sub>N<sub>2</sub>O +
H]<sup>+</sup> was assigned to the metabolite nigragilin.
Its
tandem mass spectrum displays <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 129.1386 [C<sub>7</sub>H<sub>17</sub>N<sub>2</sub> + H]<sup>+</sup> corresponding to <em>N</em>-methylpiperazinium. This ion
resulted from the loss of the side chain (94 Da). Another fragment
at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 95.0494 [C<sub>6</sub>H<sub>7</sub>O]<sup>+</sup> was assigned as an acylium ion, formed through
α-cleavage of the C–N bond of the amide functionality
and elimination of the <em>N</em>-methylpiperazine moiety.</p>
<p>The ion at tR 5.04 min with <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 323.0550 [C<sub>18</sub>H<sub>12</sub>O<sub>6</sub> – H]<sup>−</sup> present only in BP_An28d and BPS_An28d was annotated
as atromentin. Its MS<sup>2</sup> spectrum showed the fragment ion <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 295.0615 [C<sub>17</sub>H<sub>12</sub>O<sub>5</sub> – H]<sup>−</sup> yielded after the precursor
lost CO (28 Da).</p>
<p>The peak at tR 6.15 min with <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 379.0450 [C<sub>20</sub>H<sub>12</sub>O<sub>8</sub> – H]<sup>−</sup>, present in the three cultivation
times, was assigned
as asperlone B. In its tandem mass spectrum, <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 361.0362 [C<sub>20</sub>H<sub>10</sub>O<sub>7</sub> –
H]<sup>−</sup> and 333.0400 [C<sub>19</sub>H<sub>10</sub>O<sub>6</sub> – H]<sup>−</sup> were observed, consistent
with the loss of H<sub>2</sub>O (18 Da) and CO (28 Da), respectively.</p>
<p>The metabolite pestalamide A, known as an <em>A. niger-</em>derived compound, was annotated in positive mode with <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 344.1132 [C<sub>18</sub>H<sub>18</sub>NO<sub>6</sub> + H]<sup>+</sup>. Its MS<sup>2</sup> spectrum showed a fragment <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> = 230.0826 [C<sub>13</sub>H<sub>10</sub>NO<sub>3</sub> + H]<sup>+</sup>. This ion was produced after the
precursor lost the 2-methylbutane-dioic acid moiety. The ion <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 230.0826 in turn produced the lighter
ions <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 213.0548, 185.0512, and
157.0647 after eliminating NH<sub>3</sub> (17 Da), NH<sub>3</sub>/CO
(45 Da), and NH<sub>3</sub>/2CO (73 Da), respectively. In negative
mode, the metabolite pestalamide A (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 342.0970) gave fragment ions <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 228.0634 [C<sub>13</sub>H<sub>11</sub>NO<sub>3</sub> – H]<sup>−</sup> and <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 185.0620
[C<sub>12</sub>H<sub>10</sub>O<sub>2</sub> – H]<sup>−</sup> in its MS/MS spectrum. These fragments corresponded to sequential
elimination of the 2-methylbutane-dioic acid moiety and NH<sub>3</sub> (17 Da), respectively.</p>
<p>The peak at tR 6.38 min with <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 213.1126 [C<sub>11</sub>H<sub>18</sub>O<sub>8</sub> – H]<sup>−</sup> in negative mode was
attributed to hexylitaconic acid.
In its MS<sup>2</sup> spectrum, the observed fragment <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 169.1219 [C<sub>10</sub>H<sub>18</sub>O<sub>2</sub> – H]<sup>−</sup> differs from the precursor by CO<sub>2</sub> (44 Da).</p>
<p>The peak at tR 7.98 min with <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 339.2511 [C<sub>18</sub>H<sub>36</sub>O<sub>4</sub> + Na]<sup>+</sup> and 315.2531 [C<sub>18</sub>H<sub>36</sub>O<sub>4</sub> –
H]<sup>−</sup>, in positive and negative mode, respectively,
was annotated as 9,10-dihydroxystearic acid. In the negative mode,
the obtained fragment <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 297.2421
[C<sub>18</sub>H<sub>34</sub>O<sub>3</sub> – H]<sup>−</sup> corresponded to the loss of H<sub>2</sub>O (18 Da).</p>
<p>Starch
was added to the culture media as a carbohydrate source
to enhance the fungal catabolism and metabolism. Out of this process,
99 secondary metabolites were detected and are reported in <a href="#tbl2" class="usa-link">Table 
</a>. Part of these compounds
were plant metabolites and were annotated as hexadecasphinganine (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 274.2744), an alkylarylamine (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 304.3005), 1,10-epoxy-8alfa-methoxyvermophilanolide
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 279.1597), an isomer of Val-Val-Lys-Lys
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 473.3453), a peptide (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 517.3712), linolenic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 279.2322), 2-aminohexadec-11-en-3-ol
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 256.2641) alongside isoorientin
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 471.1050), eriodyctiol-6-C-beta-<span class="font-variant-small-caps">d</span>-glucopyranoside (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 473.1027),
etalon (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 391.2848), and cycloeucalenone
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 425.3781). Part of the metabolites
found exclusively in banana peel-derived media were not detected in
those composed of starch-enriched banana peel. On the other hand,
these starch-enriched media also yielded compounds that were not detected
in media without starch. This discrepancy can be associated with microorganism
biochemical activities that change according to the chemical properties
of the culture medium. In this way, the
medium chemical composition modulates different metabolism activities.
Growing <em>A. niger</em> on starch-enriched banana peel media
afforded 22 secondary metabolites previously identified in the same
species; 19 additional compounds herein reported were already found
in other <em>Aspergillus</em> species (with genomes either
fully or partially identified). In addition, 25 of these fungus-derived
metabolites were already described in mushrooms, bacteria, fungi,
association fungi/bacteria, and algae species. Among the previously
reported <em>A. niger</em> secondary metabolites, 18 were
found in both media. However, tensyuic acid A (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 243.0867), the peptide (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 625.2447), aspernigrin C (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 571.2076),
and tensyuic acid D (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 295.1158)
were generated only in starch-enriched banana peel media.</p>
<p>In
banana peel media, the biosynthesis of previously identified
metabolites also occurred. These compounds included 9-hydroxyhexylitaconic
acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 229.1070), penicillenol
C1 (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 294.1698), brevianamide
M (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 322.1180), pulvinic acid
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 309.0762), asperfuranone (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 377.1590), and aspergilloid E (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 341.1616) previously reported in <em>Aspergillus</em> species with the fully identified genome.<em>
−




</em>
<a href="#tbl2" class="usa-link">Table 
</a> also displays
substances previously found in <em>Aspergillus</em> species
with partially identified genomes. Two of these compounds, namely,
asperlone B (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 379.0445) and sch725674
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 351.2143), were biosynthesized
in both cultures, whereas aspergilactone A (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 213.1128), purpurquinone A (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 455.0969), phomoligol A (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 329.1231), β-<span class="font-variant-small-caps">d</span>-glucopyranosyl aspergillusense A (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 441.2113), asperlone B (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 379.0445), JBIR-138 (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 363.1438), aspertetranone C (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 421.1856), mitorubrinic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 435.0713), carnemycin B (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 451.1954), and 2-aminohexadec-11-en-3-ol (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 256.2641) were found only in starch-enriched media. These
culture media also produced harzianopyridone (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 282.1344), which was previously identified in <em>Trichoderma harzianum</em>. In addition,
1,2-diacetoxy-3-(4′hydroxyphenyl)-4,7,8-trihydroxydibenzofuran
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 425.0866), roridinic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 301.1281), hamigeromycin A (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 393.1539), and macrosphelide I (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 343.1387) were formerly identified
in the mushroom <em>Sarcodon laevigatum</em>
 and the fungi <em>Cordyceps</em> sp.,
<em>Hamigera avellamea</em>, and <em>Periconia byssoides</em>. Atromentic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 341.0660) from <em>Boletus permagnificus</em>, inoscavin B (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 437.1229) from <em>Innonotus xeranticus</em>, [3-hydroxy-4-(6-methoxy-5,8-
dioxonaphthalen-2-yl)-5-oxofuran-2-ylidene]­(4-hydroxy phenyl)­acetic
acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 435.0708) from <em>Xerocomus badius</em>, pyrenocine
L (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 307.1158), 2-hexylidene-3-methylsuccinic acid – methyl ester
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 227.1280) from <em>Halorosellinia
oceanica</em>, myxotrichin C (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 493.1131) from <em>Myxotrichum
spp</em>
 colletobredin C (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 427.2324) from <em>Colletotrichum
Aotearoa</em>, herqueichrysin (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 357.1336) from <em>Penicillium
herquei</em>, talaroketal A (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 627.1865) from <em>Talaromyces
stipitatus</em>, trichoflectin (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 299.0920) from <em>Trichopezizella
nidulus</em>, salinipyrone A (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 315.1571) from <em>Salinispora
pacifica</em>, and botcinic acid
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 425.2150) from <em>Botrytis
cinerea</em>
 were also annotated
in this second medium. The structures of 26 detected compounds could
not be characterized.</p>
<p>The GNPS database was also used to annotate
the metabolites and
correlate those of the same classes using molecular networks. In this
technique, each compound is represented by a node and each node contains
a pizza diagram according to its proportion in different analyzed
samples. The network connects substances that are stereoisomers, positional,
and functional isomers.
</p>
<p>GNPS analysis
of BP_An14d, BP_An21d, and BP_An28d detected 45 nodes;
among them, 28 match substances in its database (<a href="#fig5" class="usa-link">Figure 
</a>). In the positive mode ionization,
the plant secondary metabolites isoorientin and eriodictyol-6-C-β-<span class="font-variant-small-caps">d</span>-glucopyranoside form a cluster as both differ by one unsaturation.
Although six bicoumarins were detected and identified (<a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">Tables 
</a> and ), only kotanin, isokotanin B, and orlandin were detected in GNPS.
These metabolites formed a cluster as they are structurally related
and differ from one another by the number of methoxy groups. Two alkamides,
including 2-methyl-<em>N</em>-(2-phenylethyl)­propanamide and
2-methyl-<em>N</em>-(2-phenylethyl)­butanamide, also formed
a cluster, as the difference between both compounds is 14 Da (methyl
group). Other compounds detected by GNPS did not form clusters, including
those of the negative mode ionization. The MS-FINDER database proposed
the structure of aurasperone A for <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 571.1605, which was found in 21 and 28 days of fermentation. However,
GNPS identified for the same mass value, a biflavonoid known as 4′,4′′′-di-O-methylisochamaejasmin.
This database also revealed that <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 571.1605 is present in banana peels.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig5"><h4 class="obj_head">5.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12878763_ao5c10614_0005.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/bd61/12878763/41353052053d/ao5c10614_0005.jpg" loading="lazy" id="gr5" height="1049" width="758" alt="5"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig5/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Molecular network from
positive (ESI+) and negative (ESI−)
ionization modes of extracts from <em>A. niger</em> grown
on banana peel (BP), for 14, 21, and 28 days. Where yellow = BP, orange
= 14 days, purple = 21 days, and green = 28 days.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Analysis of BPS_An14d, BPS_An21d, and BPS_An28d
led to the detection
of 45 nodes; among them, 20 were matched secondary metabolites in
the GNPS database (<a href="#fig6" class="usa-link">Figure 
</a>). Like the above-mentioned analysis, a cluster was formed
among the two flavonoids, isoorientin and eriodictyol-6-C-β-<span class="font-variant-small-caps">d</span>-glucopyranoside. A group was also formed by cylcoeucalenone,
botcinic acid, and an unidentified compound. However, the combination
might be due to the misinterpretation of the database, since one is
a triterpene and the other is a polyketide. Part of the metabolites
composed of flavasperone (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 287.0920),
hexylcitric acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 275.1126),
JBIR-138 (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 363.1438), β-<span class="font-variant-small-caps">d</span>-glucopyranosyl aspergillusene A (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 441.2113), and the peptide related to Glu-Arg-Phe (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 449.2135) was found only in this analysis.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig6"><h4 class="obj_head">6.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12878763_ao5c10614_0006.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/bd61/12878763/52dd1584ec51/ao5c10614_0006.jpg" loading="lazy" id="gr6" height="1313" width="789" alt="6"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig6/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Molecular
network from positive (ESI+) and negative (ESI−)
ionization modes of extracts from <em>A. niger</em> grown
on banana peel with starch (BPS), for 14, 21, and 28 days. Where in
yellow = BP, orange = 14 days, purple = 21 days, and green = 28 days.</p></figcaption></figure><p>The studied fungus was also cultured on starch
for 14, 21, and
28 days, affording Starch_An14d, Starch_An21d, and Starch_An28d, respectively
(<a href="#tbl3" class="usa-link">Table 
</a>). LCMS analyses led to the detection of 41 compounds;
among them, 29 were characterized. The fungus on starch medium produced
fewer secondary metabolites than when grown on BP and BPS. Fifteen
of these metabolites were already identified from other <em>Aspergillus</em> species, while eight were unique to <em>A. niger</em> and
four to other species (<a href="#fig7" class="usa-link">Figure 
</a>). The GNPS analyses depicted a node between the unidentified
metabolites <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 430.3890, 428.3730,
416.3730, and 402.3579, a sphingolipid identified as <em>N</em>-(propionyl)-4-hydroxyeicosasphinganine. The first metabolite (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> = 430.3890) differs from the sphingolipid
by 18 Da, indicating the presence of an OH group. Then, the metabolite <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 428.3730 has a double-bond equivalence
less than that of the first metabolite. The last metabolite (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 416.3730) is 14 Da higher than the
sphingolipid, indicating a longer side chain by an additional CH<sub>2</sub> group. GNPS obtained from ESI­(+) identified kotanin (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> = 439.1380) and two related bicoumarins
at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> = 425.1230 (demethylkotanin)
and 411.1380 (orlandin) in the same cluster. This analysis also depicted
three steroids <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 509.2750 (asperterpene
I), 525.271, and 493.2800 forming a cluster the same as the sphingolipids
at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 274.2750, 318.3010, and 362.3260.
Aurasperone A (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 571.159) and
fonsecinone B (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 589.171) differ
by 18 Da, despite no node being observed between them.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig7"><h4 class="obj_head">7.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12878763_ao5c10614_0007.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/bd61/12878763/4d58d212a258/ao5c10614_0007.jpg" loading="lazy" id="gr7" height="1035" width="759" alt="7"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig7/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Molecular network extracts in positive (ESI+) and negative (ESI−)
ionization modes of <em>A. niger</em> grown on starch for
14, 21, and 28 days, where orange = 14 days, purple = 21 days, and
green = 28 days.</p></figcaption></figure><p>GNPS obtained from ESI(−)
also revealed
the presence of
polyhydroxylated fatty acids, a fatty diacid, and two aliphatic polyketides.
In addition to two naphthopyrones, a naphthopyranone, a bicoumarin,
an isocoumarin, and a terphenyl derivative were also characterized.
A macrolide with a structure related to the five others was found.</p>
<p>The growth of a microorganism depends on the nutritional value
of the culture medium, alongside other factors, such as pH, temperature,
time of cultivation, and solid/liquid conditions. Therefore, the modification
of one of these parameters can affect or promote growth with variation
in metabolite profiles.
</p>
<p>Banana
peels and starch media yielded seven identical compounds,
namely, similanpyrone B (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 205.0503),
orlandin (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 409.0921), thelephoric
acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 351.0138), hexylitaconic
acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 213.1129), fonsecinone
B (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 589.1703), aurasperone A
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 571.1610), and Psiaα
(<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 293.2112). Only three compounds
(similanpyrone B, orlandin, and hexylitaconic acid) were simultaneously
found in starch-enriched banana peel and starch media.</p>
<p>Comparison
of secondary metabolite profiles from both culture media
revealed that BP_An medium produces more chemomarkers (27 compounds)
from <em>A. niger</em> than the starch-enriched medium (22
compounds) (<a href="#fig8" class="usa-link">Figures 
</a> and ). This latter promoted significantly
the production of chemo-markers (19 compounds) already reported in
the <em>Aspergillus</em> genus than BP_An medium (14 compounds).
The increase in the carbohydrate amount was beneficial to produce
more secondary metabolites (87 compounds in BPS_An against 66 in BP).
The BPS_An medium generated 26 nonidentified (NI) compounds, while
BP_An produced half of this number.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig8"><h4 class="obj_head">8.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12878763_ao5c10614_0008.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/bd61/12878763/91e9be66aa79/ao5c10614_0008.jpg" loading="lazy" id="gr8" height="491" width="750" alt="8"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig8/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Illustration of the origin distribution
across various species
for the secondary metabolites produced in BP media (BP_An14d, BP_An21d,
and BP_An28d).</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig9"><h4 class="obj_head">9.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12878763_ao5c10614_0009.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/bd61/12878763/2865cb5407d3/ao5c10614_0009.jpg" loading="lazy" id="gr9" height="458" width="695" alt="9"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig9/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Distribution of the secondary metabolites produced
in
BPS media
(BPS_An14d, BPS_An21d, and BPS_An28d) among various species.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Part of the NI metabolites did not produce sufficient
fragment
ions, enabling their characterization, and others could not be matched
either with a known compound or a class of compounds. NI compounds
might be produced after the activation of silent gene expression.
Also, during the study, four triterpenes were detected, among them
two with <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 457.3679 (14, 21, and
28 days) and two with <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 425.3776
(14 and 28 days). These compounds could be biotransformation products
of the triterpene cycloeucalenone found in banana peels.</p>
<p>As
shown in <a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">Tables 
</a>–, some of the compounds from the
cultures are produced by <em>A. niger</em> for the first time,
although already identified in other microorganisms. The variation
in these chemical profiles suggests that the studied <em>A. niger</em> may have undergone gene alteration. Fungi generate an array of natural
compounds, and these products can change if the fungi undergo mutations
caused by stress. An earlier study showed that sodium azide induced
mutagenic changes in <em>A. niger</em>, which were monitored
using RAPD-PCR analysis. One of the strategies
to mutate these genes and promote one-strain-many-compounds (OSMAC)
is to systematically change the culture conditions.<em>
,
</em> It is also noteworthy to point out that banana peel secondary metabolites
could interfere with gene expression or deletion. Isoorientin, a flavonoid
from banana peels, possesses antimicrobial activity and is known to
cause mitochondrial dysfunction in cancer cells.<em>
,
</em> Thus, its presence in the culture medium could compel the fungus
to develop adaptive mechanisms that include gene expression. The second
flavonoid, eriodictyol-6-<em>C</em>-β-<span class="font-variant-small-caps">d</span>-glucopyranoside,
was not studied for its antifungal activity. However, previous work
suggested that flavonoids are part of the plant metabolites produced
in response to fungal infection. In addition,
banana peel extract from an incompletely identified <em>Musa</em> species showed antifungal activity by inhibiting 72% of <em>A. niger</em> growth.
</p>
<p>The
present work used a food residue (banana peels) as a biomass
to culture <em>A. niger</em>. During the investigation, some
metabolites were produced, as depicted in <a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">Tables 
</a>–. Part of
these metabolites showed pharmacological properties in previous studies.
Aurasperone displayed IC<sub>50</sub> of 12.25 μM and antiviral
activity against SARS-CoV-2. It also
presents a high selectivity index of 2641.5. This compound also proved
to be an antioxidant and was shown to possess a protective effect
on CHO cell lines against H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>. Hexylitaconic acid inhibited with IC<sub>50</sub> of 50
μg/mL the human double minute 2 (HDM2), a protein overexpressed
in many human cancers that interferes with the tumor-suppressing functionality
of p53. This compound also promoted up
to 13 and 66.3% of the germination and biomass in cauliflower seedlings. Pyranonigrin A showed anti-inflammatory activity
by reducing the level of NO production.<em>
,
</em>
</p>
<p>Banana
peel is rich in nutrients, including minerals, carbohydrates
(xylose, pectin, cellulose), amino acids, and fatty acids<em>
,
</em> that could favor the growth of <em>A. niger</em>. However,
it also contains secondary metabolites that possess antimicrobial
activity, causing a strong stress to the fungus growth.<em>
,
</em> The chemical profile of the peel’s crude extract showed the
presence of secondary metabolites, namely, isoorientin, eriodictyol
6-C-β-<span class="font-variant-small-caps">d</span>-glucopyranoside, hexadecasphinganine, 1,10-epoxy-8α-methoxyermophilanolide,
and di­(2-ethylhexyl)­phthalate (<a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">Tables 
</a> and ). Isoorientin was found
in an ethanolic extract of <em>Mentha arvensis</em> that displayed
strong antibacterial activity against bacteria from the ESKAPE consortium. In addition, this metabolite showed strong antimicrobial
and antibiofilm activity against <em>E. coli</em> by interacting
with the extracellular polymeric substances. Flavonoids with an antibiofilm property also affected the <em>A. flavus</em> cell wall by causing osmotic stress as shown in
a previous report. In response to this stress, the fungus increased
its production of ergosterol. In addition, <em>A. flavus</em> exposure to <span class="font-variant-small-caps">d</span>-glucal led to the suppression
of aflatoxin biosynthesis and greatly increased kojic acid production. The stress induced by banana peels’ secondary
metabolites might explain the presence of wall cell metabolites such
as cholesterol, polyhydroxylated fatty acid derivatives, and compounds
not previously found in the studied fungus.</p>
<p>The starch-enriched
banana peel medium yielded more metabolites,
with 28 days of fermentation proving to be the optimal condition.
Sabat and co-workers similarly found antifungal profiles against <em>Verticillium wilt</em> of <em>Penicillium steckii</em> extracts produced on different carbohydrate-enriched media. Vandermolen and co-workers also demonstrated
that three fungal strains presented different secondary metabolite
profiles after growing them separately on rice, grits, oatmeal, and
wheat germ flour. Other previous studies
revealed that to overcome stress caused by pH, nutrients, temperature,
osmosis, and oxidative agents, fungi can increase, suppress, and promote
the synthesis of some secondary metabolites.<em>
,
</em>
</p>
<p>Overall, the presence of banana peels in BP and BPS media
seemed
to increase the production of fungal secondary metabolites. Banana
peels, secondary metabolites, seem to play a key role in the expression
of genes that regulate several biosynthesis pathways.</p></section></section><section id="sec4"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">4. Conclusion</h2>
<p>Suitable management of food
residue and waste can bring numerous
beneficial outcomes, such as renewable energy and valuable biomolecules
with applications in drug discovery, veterinary medicine, and agriculture.
Thus, the present study aimed to unveil biomolecules produced by <em>A. niger</em> when grown on banana peels (BP) used as biomass.
The study also enriched BP (BPS) with starch to understand the chemical
changes that can occur when the fungus grows in this medium. The combination
of in silico statistical tools (metaboanalyst) and databases for dereplication
(GNPS, MS-FINDER, and MS-DIAL) enabled us to establish and compare
the chemical profile according to cultivation periods (14, 21, and
28 days) and culture medium. This unprecedented study reveals that
the chemical profiles of <em>A. niger</em> grown on BP and
BPS were composed of 76 and 99 substances, respectively. Alongside
banana peel metabolites, polyketides, cyclopeptides, citric acid derivatives,
alkamides, and sesquiterpenes were characterized, among others. Statistical
analysis revealed that banana peel medium can produce secondary metabolites
with pharmacological potential independent of the fermentation period.
However, the starch-enriched banana peel medium optimally produced
fungal compounds during the 28-day fermentation.</p>
<p>The present
study provides a cheap process to induce and modulate
fungal valuable molecule production and a method to turn waste into
valuable products.</p></section><section id="sec16"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Supplementary Material</h2>
